14:T4cd,Cost per token varies enormously across providers, and Cline's agentic loop compounds that variance quickly — a single task can involve many tool calls, each resending accumulated context, so a model that looks cheap per request can still produce a surprisingly large bill over a long session. Context window matters independently of cost: Cline enforces its own hard 300KB limit on any single file read regardless of what the underlying model technically supports, so a model's enormous advertised context window doesn't remove every practical constraint you'll run into, though it does still matter enormously for how much conversation history and how many open files Cline can reason about before needing to trim older context. Diff-edit reliability is the trade-off that gets talked about least but affects daily usability the most: some models are simply more consistent at producing diffs that match Cline's expected format exactly, especially on larger files or late in a long session, and a model that's technically more capable but less consistent at this specific mechanical task can end up costing you more time in failed edits than a slightly less 'smart' model that reliably gets the format right on the first try.15:T52e,The provider category you choose is a separate decision from which specific model within that category, and it has real implications beyond raw performance. Proprietary frontier models, accessed through a vendor API, generally deliver the strongest reasoning and the most consistent tool-calling behavior, at the cost of paying per token and sending your code to a third party with every request. Open-weight models — families like DeepSeek and Qwen, among others — have closed much of the capability gap for coding tasks specifically, cost meaningfully less per token when accessed through an API, and can be self-hosted entirely if you're willing to manage the infrastructure, which matters if your organization has data residency or compliance requirements that rule out sending code to an external API at all. Fully local models running through Ollama sit at the far end of that spectrum: zero per-token cost and complete data privacy, in exchange for needing capable hardware and generally accepting a real capability gap against the current frontier, particularly on complex reasoning-heavy tasks. None of these three categories is universally correct — they're a genuine trade-off between capability, cost, and control, and the right answer depends on what your specific workflow and constraints actually require.16:T475,Prompt caching, where a provider charges a reduced rate for reprocessing context that hasn't changed since the previous request, matters enormously for Cline specifically because its agentic loop repeatedly resends large chunks of the same conversation and file context across many tool calls within a single task. A model without effective caching support can end up costing several times more than its raw per-token price suggests once you account for how much of that price is paying to reprocess the same context over and over. Extended or adjustable reasoning modes, offered by several providers as a toggle rather than a separate model, let you dial reasoning depth up for a genuinely hard architectural question and back down for routine implementation work within the same model family, which is often a more precise lever than switching models entirely. Checking whether your chosen provider supports both of these before assuming a model is 'too expensive' for a given task is worth the five minutes it takes, since the effective cost of the same model can vary several-fold depending on whether these settings are configured well.17:T42c,The most expensive mistake is running every task through the single most capable model available, on the assumption that more capability can never hurt. In practice it mostly just means paying premium rates for tasks a cheaper model would have handled identically, since a model's advantage tends to concentrate in specific kinds of reasoning-heavy work rather than applying uniformly across everything you ask it to do. The second common mistake is the opposite: sticking with whatever free or cheap model got you started even after a project has grown complex enough that its architecture decisions genuinely warrant stronger reasoning, which quietly produces worse decisions that cost far more time to unwind later than the premium model would have cost upfront. The third is ignoring Plan and Act as a lever entirely and running both modes on the same single model by default, which forfeits one of the more effective cost and quality optimizations Cline actually offers without requiring any extra setup complexity beyond the initial few minutes of configuration.18:T550,Benchmark charts are genuinely useful, but the same skepticism worth applying to any statistic circulating in a fast-moving space applies here too. A benchmark score tells you how a model performed on a specific, fixed set of test problems under specific conditions — it doesn't tell you how that model handles your particular codebase's conventions, your team's coding style, or the specific mix of languages and frameworks you actually work in day to day. Two models can post nearly identical scores on a general coding benchmark while behaving noticeably differently inside Cline specifically, because Cline's agentic tool-calling loop stresses a model in ways a single-shot benchmark question simply doesn't test. Treat published rankings as a reasonable starting shortlist rather than a final verdict, and weight your own experience running a model on real tasks in your actual project more heavily than a leaderboard position from a benchmark you'll never personally use the same way it was tested. The community-usage-pattern data Cline itself has published — showing real concentration in which models developers actually choose for Plan versus Act across thousands of real sessions — is a genuinely more useful signal than a synthetic benchmark score precisely because it reflects real task performance rather than a controlled test environment.19:T983,هزینهٔ هر توکن در سراسر ارائه‌دهندگان بسیار متفاوت است، و حلقهٔ عاملی Cline آن تنوع را سریع انباشته می‌کند — یک وظیفهٔ منفرد می‌تواند شامل فراخوانی‌های ابزار زیادی باشد، هرکدام زمینهٔ انباشته را دوباره می‌فرستند، پس مدلی که به‌ازای هر درخواست ارزان به نظر می‌رسد همچنان می‌تواند صورت‌حسابی به‌طرز شگفت‌آوری بزرگ در طول یک نشست بلند تولید کند. پنجرهٔ زمینه مستقل از هزینه اهمیت دارد: Cline سقف سخت‌گیرانهٔ ۳۰۰ کیلوبایتی خودش را روی هر خواندن فایل منفرد اعمال می‌کند، فارغ از آن‌چه مدل زیربنایی از نظر فنی پشتیبانی می‌کند، پس پنجرهٔ زمینهٔ عظیم تبلیغ‌شدهٔ یک مدل هر محدودیت عملی‌ای را که با آن روبه‌رو می‌شوید حذف نمی‌کند، هرچند همچنان برای این‌که چقدر تاریخچهٔ مکالمه و چند فایل باز Cline می‌تواند پیش از نیاز به کوتاه‌کردن زمینهٔ قدیمی‌تر دربارهٔ آن‌ها استدلال کند، بسیار اهمیت دارد. قابلیت‌اعتماد ویرایش دیف مصالحه‌ای است که کمترین صحبت دربارهٔ آن می‌شود اما بیشترین تأثیر را بر قابلیت‌استفادهٔ روزانه می‌گذارد: برخی مدل‌ها به‌سادگی در تولید دیف‌هایی که دقیقاً با فرمت مورد انتظار Cline مطابقت دارند ثابت‌تر هستند، به‌خصوص روی فایل‌های بزرگ‌تر یا در اواخر یک نشست طولانی، و مدلی که از نظر فنی توانمندتر است اما در این وظیفهٔ مکانیکی خاص کمتر ثابت است می‌تواند در نهایت زمان بیشتری در ویرایش‌های شکست‌خورده نسبت به مدلی که کمی «باهوش‌تر» نیست اما فرمت را در اولین تلاش به‌طور قابل‌اعتماد درست می‌گیرد، به شما هزینه بدهد.1a:T8ba,دستهٔ ارائه‌دهنده‌ای که انتخاب می‌کنید تصمیمی جداگانه از این‌که کدام مدل خاص درون آن دسته است می‌باشد، و پیامدهای واقعی فراتر از عملکرد خام دارد. مدل‌های پیشرفتهٔ اختصاصی، که از طریق API یک فروشنده دسترسی‌پذیرند، عموماً قوی‌ترین استدلال و ثابت‌ترین رفتار فراخوانی ابزار را ارائه می‌دهند، به قیمت پرداخت به‌ازای هر توکن و ارسال کد شما به یک شخص‌ثالث با هر درخواست. مدل‌های وزن-باز — خانواده‌هایی مانند DeepSeek و Qwen، در میان دیگران — بخش زیادی از شکاف توانمندی را مشخصاً برای وظایف کدنویسی بسته‌اند، هنگام دسترسی از طریق API به‌طور معناداری هر توکن ارزان‌تر هستند، و اگر مایل به مدیریت زیرساخت باشید می‌توانند کاملاً خودمیزبانی شوند، که اگر سازمان شما الزامات محل‌سکونت داده یا انطباق دارد که ارسال کد به یک API خارجی را کاملاً منتفی می‌کند، اهمیت دارد. مدل‌های کاملاً محلی که از طریق Ollama اجرا می‌شوند، در انتهای دور آن طیف قرار می‌گیرند: هزینهٔ صفر هر توکن و حریم خصوصی کامل داده، در ازای نیاز به سخت‌افزار توانمند و به‌طور کلی پذیرش یک شکاف توانمندی واقعی در برابر خط مقدم فعلی، به‌خصوص در وظایف استدلال‌محور پیچیده. هیچ‌کدام از این سه دسته به‌طور جهانی درست نیست — آن‌ها یک مصالحهٔ واقعی میان توانمندی، هزینه و کنترل هستند، و پاسخ درست به این بستگی دارد که جریان کاری و محدودیت‌های خاص شما واقعاً به چه چیزی نیاز دارند.1b:T7d0,کش‌کردن پرامپت، جایی که یک ارائه‌دهنده نرخ کاهش‌یافته‌ای برای پردازش مجدد زمینه‌ای که از درخواست قبلی تغییر نکرده دریافت می‌کند، مشخصاً برای Cline بسیار اهمیت دارد چون حلقهٔ عاملی آن مکرراً بخش‌های بزرگی از همان زمینهٔ مکالمه و فایل را در سراسر فراخوانی‌های ابزار زیادی درون یک وظیفهٔ منفرد دوباره می‌فرستد. مدلی بدون پشتیبانی مؤثر از کش می‌تواند در نهایت چندین برابر قیمت خام هر توکن آن هزینه داشته باشد، وقتی در نظر بگیرید چقدر از آن قیمت صرف پردازش مجدد همان زمینه بارها و بارها می‌شود. حالت‌های استدلال گسترده یا قابل‌تنظیم، که چند ارائه‌دهنده به‌عنوان یک کلید به‌جای یک مدل جداگانه ارائه می‌دهند، به شما اجازه می‌دهند عمق استدلال را برای یک سؤال معماری واقعاً سخت بالا ببرید و برای کار پیاده‌سازی روتین درون همان خانوادهٔ مدل پایین بیاورید، که اغلب اهرم دقیق‌تری نسبت به سوئیچ‌کردن کامل مدل است. بررسی این‌که آیا ارائه‌دهندهٔ انتخابی شما از هر دوی این‌ها پشتیبانی می‌کند پیش از فرض این‌که یک مدل برای یک وظیفهٔ خاص «خیلی گران» است، ارزش پنج دقیقه‌ای که می‌گیرد را دارد، چون هزینهٔ مؤثر همان مدل می‌تواند بسته به این‌که این تنظیمات چقدر خوب پیکربندی شده‌اند چند برابر متفاوت باشد.1c:T8fe,نمودارهای معیار واقعاً مفیدند، اما همان شکاکیتی که ارزش اعمال روی هر آماری که در یک فضای سریع‌التغییر گردش می‌کند را دارد، این‌جا هم صدق می‌کند. یک نمرهٔ معیار به شما می‌گوید یک مدل روی مجموعه‌ای خاص و ثابت از مسائل تست تحت شرایط خاص چگونه عمل کرده — به شما نمی‌گوید آن مدل چگونه با قراردادهای کدبیس خاص شما، سبک کدنویسی تیم‌تان، یا ترکیب خاصی از زبان‌ها و چارچوب‌هایی که واقعاً روزانه با آن‌ها کار می‌کنید رفتار می‌کند. دو مدل می‌توانند نمرات تقریباً یکسانی روی یک معیار کدنویسی عمومی ثبت کنند درحالی‌که به‌طور محسوسی متفاوت درون Cline رفتار می‌کنند، چون حلقهٔ فراخوانی ابزار عاملی Cline یک مدل را به شکل‌هایی تحت فشار می‌گذارد که یک سؤال معیار تک‌شات به‌سادگی تست نمی‌کند. رتبه‌بندی‌های منتشرشده را یک فهرست کوتاه اولیهٔ معقول در نظر بگیرید نه یک حکم نهایی، و به تجربهٔ خودتان از اجرای یک مدل روی وظایف واقعی در پروژهٔ واقعی‌تان وزن بیشتری بدهید تا یک جایگاه در جدول رتبه‌بندی از معیاری که هرگز شخصاً به همان شکلی که تست شده استفاده نخواهید کرد. دادهٔ الگوی مصرف جامعه‌ای که خود Cline منتشر کرده — نشان‌دهندهٔ تمرکز واقعی در این‌که توسعه‌دهندگان واقعاً کدام مدل‌ها را برای Plan در برابر Act در سراسر هزاران نشست واقعی انتخاب می‌کنند — سیگنالی واقعاً مفیدتر از یک نمرهٔ معیار مصنوعی است، دقیقاً چون عملکرد واقعی وظیفه را منعکس می‌کند نه یک محیط تست What Exactly Happened — The Timeline Without Spin"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"SpaceX and Cursor first announced a strategic partnership in April 2026. On August 14 the deal closed. Two weeks later OpenAI exercised the change-of-control clause in its custom contract with Cursor."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"OpenAI’s official post is short. It states that the company notified SpaceX of its intent to wind down the contract, proposed November 12 as the shutoff date, and gave the maximum notice the contract allowed. Why OpenAI Walked Away From a Billion-Dollar Customer"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"From a pure revenue perspective the decision looks irrational. Cursor had become one of OpenAI’s top five customers by early 2026. By spring the partnership was on track to generate more than one billion dollars in annualized revenue. OpenAI still terminated it. The explanation is not revenue. It is control, competitive positioning, and legal risk management ahead of an expected public listing."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"OpenAI is preparing to go public in 2027. Investors want predictable, defensible revenue streams. Heavy dependence on a single partner controlled by a long-time adversary creates concentration risk and headline risk. By cutting Cursor off before Astra ships, OpenAI removes the possibility that its most advanced model will be reverse-engineered, distilled, or used to train competing systems inside the SpaceX/xAI stack. The company also signals to other large customers that change-of-control clauses will be enforced."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"There is a deeper structural point. Coding agents are no longer neutral wrappers around frontier models. They are becoming the primary distribution surface for model capability. Whoever owns the agent owns the developer relationship, the usage data, the fine-tuning signals, and the upsell path into enterprise seats. OpenAI decided it would rather lose short-term revenue than continue feeding a distribution channel controlled by its most vocal critic."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The move also mirrors earlier industry behavior. In 2025 Anthropic cut Windsurf off Claude after reports that OpenAI was exploring an acquisition. The same year Anthropic revoked OpenAI’s own API access after detecting internal benchmarking. Model providers have already shown they will weaponize access when ownership or competitive intent shifts. OpenAI simply applied the same logic at larger scale."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"3. What Developers Actually Lose on November 12"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Not every Cursor user is affected equally. Users who bring their own OpenAI API key can still route supported Chat and Agent requests directly. OpenAI’s Codex IDE extension also continues to work because Cursor is built on VS Code. However, features that depend on Cursor’s managed OpenAI integration — certain Tab completions, cloud agents, automations, and SDK surfaces — will lose access unless Cursor negotiates a new arrangement or routes through a third-party gateway."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Future OpenAI models, including Astra, are explicitly excluded from the existing agreement. The Structural Shift: From Model Wrappers to Platform Wars"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"For the last three years most AI coding tools were essentially sophisticated wrappers. They provided a better UI, better context management, and better scaffolding around a frontier model that lived somewhere else. The OpenAI–Cursor episode marks the end of that era. Model providers have realized that the agent layer is the real product. Whoever controls the agent controls the developer, the data, and the upgrade path."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"SpaceX’s acquisition of Cursor was never just about owning a popular editor. Anthropic is expanding Claude’s presence inside Cursor and other third-party surfaces while also building its own deeper tooling. The result is a rapid consolidation into a handful of vertically integrated platforms, each with its own preferred models, its own safety policies, and its own commercial incentives."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"For individual developers and small teams this creates a new set of trade-offs. Staying on a single platform maximizes convenience and feature velocity. Spreading across multiple agents and model providers maximizes resilience and bargaining power. What This Means for AI Coding Agent Design in 2026"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"The most important design lesson is that model access is not a permanent property of a product. It is a commercial relationship that can be revoked. Any agent architecture that hard-codes a single provider, or that assumes continuous access to the absolute frontier, is now incomplete."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Robust agents in 2026 will treat models as interchangeable backends. They will maintain a small set of primary providers, a secondary set of open-weight or local options, and clear degradation paths when a primary provider becomes unavailable or too expensive. They will also surface the provider choice to the user so that teams can enforce their own risk policies."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Personality and system-prompt design remain important, but they are secondary to the control plane. An agent that has a brilliant persona but cannot switch models without a code change is fragile. An agent that can swap backends in seconds and still maintain coherent memory and tool use is the one that survives the next cutoff."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is also why research logging and anonymous session persistence matter. Competitive and Regulatory Ripples"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"The OpenAI–Cursor decision will be studied by every other model provider and every other coding-tool company. Targets will negotiate longer notice periods or transitional licenses."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Regulators may also take interest. Then you try to run it and discover the real constraint was never the leaderboard. It was the memory on the card sitting in your machine. Qwen3.8-27B, Kimi K2.7 Code, and Gemma 4 are three of the most discussed open-weight options in 2026, and they occupy completely different hardware tiers. One of them fits a single consumer GPU. One of them does not fit anything you own. One of them scales from a phone to a workstation."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is a hardware-first comparison rather than a benchmark-first one. Benchmark scores matter, but they are almost all vendor-reported, measured on harnesses that differ between vendors, and they tell you nothing about whether the model will actually load on your machine with a usable context window. The order of operations that saves people the most time is simple: work out your memory budget, eliminate everything that cannot fit, and only then argue about which of the survivors is smarter."},{"type":"stats","items":[{"value":"0.6 GB / B","label":"Rough weight footprint per billion parameters at Q4_K_M, before context overhead"},{"value":"20-30%","label":"Extra headroom you should budget on top of weights for KV cache and context"},{"value":"24 GB","label":"The tier where Qwen3.8-27B and Gemma 4 31B become realistic at 4-bit"},{"value":"1T / 32B","label":"Kimi K2.7 Code total versus active parameters - The Question Behind the Question"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"When someone asks which local coding model is best, they are usually asking three separate questions at once, and the answers point in different directions."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The first question is which model produces the best code. The second is which model will run on the hardware they already own, at a speed that does not break their concentration. The third, which people rarely say out loud, is which model will still behave sensibly when it is driven by an agent that makes dozens of tool calls in a row rather than answering a single prompt. A model can win the first question and lose the other two badly enough that it is useless to you."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen3.8-27B, Kimi K2.7 Code, and Gemma 4 are useful precisely because they separate cleanly along these lines. Qwen3.8-27B is the current answer for someone with one good consumer GPU who wants agentic coding behaviour. Kimi K2.7 Code is genuinely open-weight and genuinely out of reach for local hardware, which makes it an API decision dressed as a self-hosting decision. Gemma 4 is a family rather than a model, and its value is that it scales down further than either of the others without falling off a cliff."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Treating them as three entries on the same list is the mistake. They are three different answers to three different constraints. The rest of this comparison walks through the memory math first, then each model in turn, then a tier-by-tier recommendation you can act on."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"2. The Memory Math to Do Before You Download Anything"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Almost every disappointing local model experience traces back to one of three arithmetic mistakes: forgetting quantization, forgetting the KV cache, or forgetting that a mixture-of-experts model still has to hold all of its weights in memory."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The weight footprint rule of thumb"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A workable planning estimate is roughly 0.6GB of memory per billion parameters at Q4_K_M quantization. A 12B model lands near 7-8GB, a 27B model near 16-18GB, a 31B model near 19-20GB. These are estimates derived from parameter count rather than measured downloads, so treat them as planning figures and verify the actual file size in the model library before you commit. Full-precision weights are a completely different conversation: Qwen3.8-27B in BF16 requires roughly 55.6GB, which is why nobody runs it that way on a desktop."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The KV cache is not optional"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Weights are the floor, not the total. The KV cache sits on top and scales with both context length and concurrency. A 27B model serving long-context requests to multiple users can double its memory footprint. This is the single most common reason a model that supposedly fits a 24GB card starts spilling to system RAM the moment someone points an agent at a large repository. Budget 20-30% headroom on top of the weight figure for context, and more if you plan to run long sessions or serve more than one request at a time."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Mixture-of-experts does not mean lightweight"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"MoE architectures reduce compute per token by routing each token to a small subset of experts. They do not reduce the memory needed to store the model. Kimi K2.7 Code activates 32 billion parameters out of roughly one trillion total, which is about 3% of the model, and that is the entire reason a trillion-parameter model can be served at competitive prices. It is not a reason it will run on your GPU. All of those weights still need to be resident somewhere. This distinction catches people out constantly, because the marketing language around MoE emphasises the active parameter count."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Estimate weights: parameter count multiplied by roughly 0.6GB at Q4_K_M.","Add 20-30% headroom for KV cache, more for long context or concurrent requests.","Check whether the model is dense or MoE - and remember MoE saves compute, not memory.","Confirm the actual package size in the model library rather than trusting the estimate.","On Apple Silicon, count unified memory the same way, and leave room for the operating system."]},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Applied to consumer hardware, this produces a fairly clean set of tiers. An 8GB card handles 7-12B models. A 16GB card handles the 14-20B class comfortably. A 24GB card is where 27-31B models become realistic at 4-bit. Beyond roughly 32GB you are into multi-GPU rigs or high-memory Mac Studios, and beyond that you are building a small server rather than a workstation."},{"type":"cta","title":"Ollama: The Engine Behind Local AI","text":"If you are new to running models locally, start with the runtime before the model. Ollama handles quantization, hardware detection, and serving with a single command.","buttonText":"Read the Ollama Primer","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/ollama-the-engine-behind-local-ai/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"3. Qwen3.8-27B: The Default Answer for a 24GB GPU"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"If you have one good consumer GPU and you want agentic coding behaviour rather than autocomplete, Qwen3.8-27B is currently the model to beat."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"What it actually is"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Alibaba announced Qwen3.8-27B on 3 August 2026 alongside the much larger Qwen3.8-Max, and released the open weights around 13-14 August under Apache 2.0. The checkpoint is dense - roughly 27.78 billion parameters - which means the full parameter count sits in VRAM and the memory math above applies directly with no MoE asterisk. It runs 64 layers with a hybrid Gated DeltaNet and Gated Attention design and a 5120 hidden dimension, carries a native 262,144-token context window extensible toward one million tokens with YaRN, and is natively multimodal: it accepts text, image, and video input."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Full BF16 weights need roughly 55.6GB. At 4-bit it runs on a single 24GB GPU, and the Ollama library lists packages around 18GB for both the standard and MLX variants. That 18GB figure is the practical number to plan around. It leaves real headroom on a 24GB card and makes the model uncomfortable but not impossible on 20GB."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"What the benchmarks claim"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen's launch card reports 61.7 on SWE-bench Pro, 73.0 on Terminal-Bench 2.1 with the Terminus harness, 90.3 on LiveCodeBench v6, 89.2 on GPQA Diamond, 79.5 on IFBench, and 84.3 on OSWorld-Verified. The generational comparison is the more interesting part: against Qwen3.6-27B, SWE-bench Pro moves from 53.5 to 61.7, Terminal-Bench from 63.4 to 73.0, QwenSWEBench from 49.3 to 79.0, and DeepSWE 1.1 from 13.3 to 42.2. Outside code, CoWorkBench rises from 61.0 to 70.7 and Agents' Last Exam pass@1 doubles from 10.6 to 20.4."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The shape of those gains is the signal. The published configuration is architecturally identical to Qwen3.6-27B - same layer layout - so the story is new weights and new post-training rather than a new architecture. Qwen trained this checkpoint to keep working through an environment across many steps, not to produce a better first response. For anyone using a local model inside an agent loop, that is the property that matters most."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The caveats that come with those numbers"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Every figure above is vendor-run. Qwen's own card is unusually transparent about conditions, which is to its credit and also a reminder of how much conditions matter. SWE-bench Pro was evaluated on a Claude Code harness at temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95, with a 256K context window. QwenSWEBench is an average of three runs with an eight-hour timeout and a 32,768 generated-token cap. Several of the headline benchmarks - QwenSWEBench, CoWorkBench, RecreationBench - are designed in-house, so their tasks and graders do not have the external auditability of a mature public suite."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One comparison in particular deserves care. Qwen reran several rival models on a corrected task set using Claude Code, but imported Claude Opus 4.6 Max's official SWE-bench Pro score rather than rerunning it. So the frequently repeated claim that a 27B open model beats Opus on agentic coding rests on figures from two different harnesses. The gap may well be real. It is not a controlled result, and a public task set does not make a vendor-run number independent."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Who should run it"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Anyone with 24GB of VRAM or 24GB-plus of unified memory on Apple Silicon who wants a local model that can drive an agent through multi-step work. Thinking is enabled by default, and the model exposes controls including reasoning_effort, the ability to disable thinking, and preserve_thinking for whether prior reasoning blocks survive into later turns. Those controls change latency, memory use, and task quality, so they are worth tuning deliberately rather than leaving at defaults."},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260916-171046-fd6673cfc3496ab8.webp","alt":"Qwen3.8-27B memory profile at BF16 versus 4-bit quantization on a single consumer GPU","caption":"Figure 2: The gap between 55.6GB at BF16 and roughly 18GB quantized is the entire reason this model is a consumer-hardware story at all.","altText":"Qwen3.8-27B memory profile at BF16 versus 4-bit quantization on a single consumer GPU"},{"type":"cta","title":"How to Choose the Right AI Model for Cline (And Stop Wasting Money on the Wrong One)","text":"Picking a model is only half the problem. Kimi K2.7 Code: Open Weights You Probably Cannot Run"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Moonshot AI's coding model is genuinely open-weight, genuinely strong, and genuinely not a local model for anyone reading this on a laptop."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The architecture, and why the headline number misleads"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Kimi K2.7 Code was released on 12 June 2026 as a coding-specialised successor to Kimi K2.6. It is a mixture-of-experts model with one trillion total parameters and 32 billion activated per token, routed across 384 experts with eight selected per token plus one shared expert. It runs 61 layers including one dense layer, uses Multi-head Latent Attention and SwiGLU activations, ships with native INT4 quantization, and carries a 256K-token context window. The weights are on Hugging Face under a Modified MIT license, and Moonshot documents deployment via vLLM, SGLang, and KTransformers."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The trillion-parameter headline is the part that gets repeated and the 32B active figure is the part that gets misread. Activating 3% of the model per token is why it can be served at roughly $4.00 per million output tokens instead of at prices a model that size would historically demand. It does not shrink the storage requirement. A representative self-hosting recipe uses a tensor-parallel size of eight, which is to say eight GPUs, with chunked prefill enabled to stop memory spiking during large prefills at 256K context. That is a server deployment, not a desktop one."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The design choice that defines it"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Thinking mode cannot be turned off. That is Moonshot's explicit bet that always-reasoning produces a more reliable coding agent, and it is a real trade-off rather than a marketing line. You cannot dial it down for a trivial task to save tokens or latency. Moonshot's counterweight is efficiency: the model reportedly uses roughly 30% fewer reasoning tokens per coding task than K2.6, which reduces output token count and improves effective throughput per request. There are also two API flavours sharing the same weights - the standard model and a highspeed variant tuned for roughly 180 tokens per second, up to 260 on short contexts, at exactly double the price on every tier."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Read the benchmark claims carefully"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Moonshot reports a 21.8% improvement on Kimi Code Bench v2 over K2.6. That benchmark is first-party, and the release-day table is the only detailed source available from the vendor. Some practitioners have already said the published numbers do not match what they see in real repositories. This is not a reason to dismiss the model - it is a reason to treat the numbers as company-reported positioning rather than an independent leaderboard result, which is exactly how the more careful coverage has labelled them."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One detail worth flagging: sources disagree about whether the MoonViT vision encoder carried over from K2.6. Some deployment write-ups state the vision encoder is absent in the coding-focused K2.7, while the model card summaries and several API listings describe it as natively multimodal with image input. If multimodality matters to your use case, verify against the current model card rather than trusting any secondary source, including this one."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"So what is it actually for?"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Kimi K2.7 Code belongs in this comparison as a boundary marker. It is what the open-weight frontier looks like in 2026: genuinely downloadable, genuinely permissively licensed, and genuinely requiring hardware that costs more than a car. For most teams the honest path is the API, and the open weights function as an escape hatch - the guarantee that you could self-host if the vendor relationship went wrong, and leverage in any conversation about pricing or data handling. That guarantee has real value. It is just not the same thing as a local model."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"It is also worth noting that Moonshot has since shipped Kimi K3, a 2.8 trillion parameter open-weight multimodal reasoning model using KDA and Attention Residuals, which moved the scale frontier again. The frontier open-weight tier - Kimi K3, GLM-5.2, DeepSeek-V4-Pro - remains out of reach on a single 128GB machine, and pretending otherwise is how people end up with a model paging to SSD at two tokens per second."},{"type":"cta","title":"Stop Paying for AI Coding Agents: The Open-Source Alternatives That Actually Work in 2026","text":"Open weights and open agents solve different problems. Gemma 4: The Family That Scales Down Without Falling Over"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Gemma 4 is the only entry here that is a family rather than a model, and that is the entire point of it."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Five sizes, one architecture story"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Google DeepMind released Gemma 4 on 2 April 2026 under Apache 2.0, initially in E2B, E4B, 26B A4B, and 31B variants, then added Gemma 4 12B Unified on 3 June 2026. The 26B A4B is a mixture-of-experts model activating 3.8 billion of 25.2 billion parameters and fits a single RTX 3090 or 4090. The 31B is dense and targets workstations. Its Ollama package is around 7.6GB, which puts it squarely in 8GB-card territory. This is an underrated practical advantage for local use, because the thing that makes a local model feel unusable is rarely quality and usually latency. If you are running llama.cpp, using the MTP drafters is one of the few free speed wins available."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Where it stands on capability"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The 31B dense model reports 85.2% on MMLU Pro, 89.2% on AIME 2026, 84.3% on GPQA Diamond, 80.0% on LiveCodeBench, and a Codeforces Elo around 2150. The Tier-by-Tier Recommendation"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Find your memory budget, then read only your row. Everything above it is aspiration and everything below it is a compromise you do not need to make."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"8GB VRAM"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"You are in the 7-12B class. Gemma 4 12B Unified at roughly 7.6GB is the strongest general-purpose pull at this tier, and it brings multimodality with it. If you want something dedicated to code, JetBrains' Mellum2-12B reports 69.9 on LiveCodeBench and is the strongest coding-specific option that fits. Do not attempt a 27B model here. Quantizing further to force it in trades away exactly the reasoning reliability you wanted the bigger model for."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"12-16GB VRAM"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The 14-20B class is comfortable. Gemma 4 12B Unified now has real context headroom rather than fitting on a technicality, and mid-size options such as mistral-small3.2:24b at around 15GB become viable if you keep context modest. This tier is where you should think hardest about context length, because it is the variable that decides whether you fit or spill."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"24GB VRAM"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is the Qwen3.8-27B tier, and it is the clearest recommendation in this entire comparison. Roughly 18GB for weights leaves genuine headroom for context. Gemma 4 31B is the alternative if you want the stronger general model, and Gemma 4 26B A4B is worth testing precisely because it lands within a few points of the 31B on several benchmarks while activating only 3.8 billion parameters, which means noticeably faster generation."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"32GB and above, and Apple Silicon"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Above 32GB you gain the ability to run 27-31B models at higher precision, or to run a 27B model with a genuinely long context rather than a nominal one. On Apple Silicon, unified memory counts the same way, with the caveat that the OS and everything else you have open take their share. A 32GB Mac runs a 27B class model well. A 64GB or 128GB Mac Studio opens up more, but the frontier open-weight models - Kimi K3, GLM-5.2, DeepSeek-V4-Pro - stay out of reach even at 128GB."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Multi-GPU servers"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is where Kimi K2.7 Code becomes a real option rather than a thought experiment, with a tensor-parallel size of eight as a representative configuration. If you are provisioning this, you already know it. For everyone else, the API is the honest answer."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["8GB: Gemma 4 12B Unified for general work, Mellum2-12B for code-only.","16GB: Gemma 4 12B with comfortable context, or a 20B-class model with restraint.","24GB: Qwen3.8-27B for agentic coding; Gemma 4 31B or 26B A4B for general strength.","32GB+: the same models with real context, or higher precision quantization.","Multi-GPU: Kimi K2.7 Code and the rest of the frontier open-weight tier."]},{"type":"cta","title":"Hugging Face: Qwen3.8-27B Model Card","text":"The primary source for the benchmark table, harness footnotes, and deployment recommendations discussed above.","buttonText":"Open the Model Card","buttonUrl":"https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"7. How to Read Vendor Benchmarks Without Being Fooled"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Every number in this article, and in almost every comparison you will read, was produced by the company that made the model. That does not make the numbers useless. It makes them evidence with a known bias."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Four habits will protect you from most of the damage. First, attribute every figure to whoever ran it. A public task set does not make a vendor-run result independent; the harness, the temperature, the context window, and the timeout all move the score. Second, separate in-house benchmarks from public ones. When a vendor designs the benchmark, the tasks, and the grader, the number tells you what the vendor optimised for, which is worth knowing but is not a capability measurement."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Third, record which judge model was used. Several benchmarks are scored by another language model, and a score judged by GPT-4o is not directly comparable to one judged by a different model or by execution. Fourth, never merge rows from different conditions into an average. A code-interpreter-enabled result and a no-code-interpreter result are different experiments, and a vision benchmark judged by one model snapshot is not the same measurement as one judged by another."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Applied to these three models, the practical conclusion is that the ordering is probably roughly right and the margins are probably wrong. Qwen3.8-27B is very likely better at agentic coding than its predecessor by a meaningful amount. Whether it genuinely beats a frontier closed model on SWE-bench Pro is not established by an imported score from a different harness. Kimi K2.7 Code is very likely a strong coding model and its self-reported 21.8% gain over K2.6 is very likely directionally real. Gemma 4's Arena Elo figures are the closest thing here to an independent signal, because Arena ratings come from human preference rather than from the vendor."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Attribute every number to the party that ran the evaluation.","Separate in-house benchmarks from established public suites.","Note the harness, temperature, context window, and token caps.","Record which model, if any, acted as judge.","Never average across different experimental conditions."]},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"8. Context Windows Are a Memory Decision, Not a Feature"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"A 262K context window on the model card and a 262K context window on your machine are different things, and the gap between them is measured in gigabytes."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"All three models advertise long context: 262,144 tokens native for Qwen3.8-27B with YaRN extension toward a million, 256K for Kimi K2.7 Code, and 256K for the larger Gemma 4 variants. None of those figures are free. The KV cache grows with the context you actually use, and it comes out of the same memory pool as the weights. A model that loads comfortably with a 8K context can fail at 128K on the same card."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This matters more for coding than for chat, because agentic coding is the use case that genuinely consumes context. An agent reading a repository, running tests, reading stack traces, and iterating will fill a long window quickly. The practical approach is to decide your working context first - how much of a codebase does the agent actually need resident? - and then size the model to leave room for it, rather than choosing the largest model that technically loads and discovering the context budget is gone."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"On the serving side, if you are running a model for more than one user or more than one session, concurrency multiplies the problem. Long-context requests from multiple clients against a 27B model can double the footprint. If you are deploying at 256K context, chunked prefill and batched-token limits are the settings that keep memory stable during large prefills rather than letting it spike."},{"type":"cta","title":"How to Fix Ollama's Most Common Errors (And Why They Happen)","text":"Most local model failures are memory, context, or daemon problems wearing a confusing error message. This guide covers the ones people hit most.","buttonText":"Read the Troubleshooting Guide","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/how-to-fix-ollamas-most-common-errors-and-why-they-happen/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"9. Licensing: What You Can Actually Ship"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Two of these three models carry permissive licenses with no user caps and no commercial restrictions. That is a bigger deal than it sounds."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen3.8-27B ships under Apache 2.0. Gemma 4 ships under Apache 2.0. Kimi K2.7 Code ships under a Modified MIT license. All three allow self-hosting and commercial use, which removes the category of legal friction that made earlier open-weight generations awkward for anything beyond experimentation - the monthly active user caps, the field-of-use restrictions, the acceptable use clauses that required a lawyer to interpret."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"If you are building a product on top of a local model, the license is a strategic input rather than a formality. Apache 2.0 weights mean you can ship, fine-tune, and distribute derived models without a licensing conversation. A Modified MIT license is permissive but the word 'modified' is doing work, so read the actual terms rather than pattern-matching on the name. And regardless of license, if you fine-tune and redistribute, keep provenance records of the base checkpoint and its version, because these models are revised more often than people expect."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"10. Behaviour Inside an Agent Loop"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Single-prompt quality and agent-loop reliability are different properties, and the second one is what you will actually feel."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"An agent asks a model to do something specific: read a file, decide what to change, call a tool, read the result, and adjust. Failure modes there are not usually about knowledge. They are about instruction-following drift over many turns, tool-call formatting that degrades as context fills, and the model losing track of what it already tried. This is why Terminal-Bench and SWE-bench style evaluations are more informative for this use case than knowledge benchmarks, and why Qwen's decision to foreground agentic evaluations in its launch card was a reasonable reflection of what the model was trained for."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Practical implications differ by model. Qwen3.8-27B has thinking on by default with adjustable reasoning_effort, and whether prior reasoning blocks are preserved across turns is a setting you control - which directly affects how much context the agent burns per step. Kimi K2.7 Code cannot have thinking disabled at all, which is a design commitment to reliability at the cost of flexibility. Gemma 4 supports function calling across the family, but if you are running the E2B or E4B sizes, expect to keep the task scope tight; small models handle narrow, well-specified steps far better than open-ended planning."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One more thing that matters and is rarely mentioned: quantization affects agent behaviour more than it affects chat quality. A model that still writes fluent prose at aggressive quantization may have lost enough precision to start producing malformed tool calls. If your agent suddenly develops a habit of emitting broken JSON, test a less aggressive quantization before blaming the harness."},{"type":"cta","title":"Claude Code vs Cline: Which AI Coding Agent Should Developers Use in 2026?","text":"The model is one half of the system. The agent harness that drives it determines how much of that capability you actually get.","buttonText":"Compare the Agents","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/claude-code-vs-cline-which-ai-coding-agent-should-developers-use-in-2026/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"11. Quantization: Where the Quality Actually Goes"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Quantization is the lever that makes all of this possible, and it is also the one most often pulled too hard."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Going from BF16 to 4-bit takes Qwen3.8-27B from roughly 55.6GB to around 18GB. That is not a small optimisation; it is the difference between a server and a desktop. The cost is precision, and precision does not degrade uniformly across capabilities. Conversational fluency survives aggressive quantization well. Arithmetic, long-chain reasoning, strict format adherence, and tool-call correctness degrade earlier and more visibly."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A reasonable default is Q4_K_M, which is where the 0.6GB-per-billion estimate comes from and which most people find to be the best quality-per-gigabyte trade. If you have headroom, moving up to a higher-precision quantization is usually a better use of memory than moving up a model size, particularly for agentic work. If you are short on memory, dropping to 3-bit to force a larger model onto the card is usually a mistake: a well-quantized smaller model beats a badly-quantized larger one for anything requiring precision."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Also check who produced the quantization. Different GGUF builds of the same model are not equivalent, and dynamic quantization approaches that preserve precision selectively in the layers that need it most have become a meaningful differentiator. It is worth trying more than one build of the same model before concluding the model itself is disappointing."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"12. A Practical Evaluation Protocol"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Benchmarks tell you about someone else's tasks. Half a day of structured testing on your own repository tells you about yours."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Pick five to ten tasks from your actual work that you have already solved, so you know what a correct answer looks like. A bug fix with a known root cause. A refactor across three or four files. A test that needs writing for existing code. A stack trace that needs diagnosing. Something that requires reading documentation you have locally. These should be representative of what you actually do, not showcase tasks."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Then run each candidate model through all of them under identical conditions: same agent harness, same context settings, same quantization tier where comparable. Record three things per task - whether the output was correct, how many turns it took, and how long it took in wall-clock time. The turn count is the most underrated metric, because a model that gets there in twelve turns costs you more context, more time, and more chances to go wrong than one that gets there in four."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Use tasks you have already solved, so correctness is unambiguous.","Hold the harness, context settings, and quantization constant across candidates.","Record correctness, turn count, and wall-clock time separately.","Test at the context length you will actually use, not a convenient short one.","Re-test when you change quantization build, not just when you change model."]},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Budget half a day for this. It is the single highest-return activity in the entire local-model decision, and it replaces an unbounded amount of forum argument with an answer that is actually about your work."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"13. Common Mistakes"},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Choosing by leaderboard position before checking whether the model fits your memory budget.","Reading MoE active-parameter counts as memory requirements - Kimi K2.7 activates 32B but stores 1T.","Forgetting the KV cache and then being surprised when a long-context session spills to system RAM.","Comparing vendor benchmark numbers across different harnesses as if they were the same experiment.","Quantizing too aggressively to fit a larger model, then blaming the model for malformed tool calls.","Running weights pulled months ago - several of these checkpoints have been revised since release.","Treating a coding-specialist model as a general assistant, or a general model as a coding specialist.","Testing with single prompts when your actual use case is a multi-turn agent loop."]},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"14. The Verdict"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Three models, three clearly different answers, and the deciding variable is hardware rather than capability."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen3.8-27B is the recommendation for anyone with 24GB of VRAM or unified memory who wants agentic coding from a local model. It was trained for exactly that, its generational gains cluster in exactly that area, and roughly 18GB of quantized weights leaves room for a workable context. The vendor benchmarks should be read as positioning rather than proof, but the direction they point is consistent with how the model behaves in practice."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Gemma 4 is the recommendation for anyone below that tier, anyone who needs multimodality, and anyone who wants one family covering multiple devices. The 12B Unified at around 7.6GB is the most useful single model for an 8GB card in 2026, the 26B A4B is the speed-conscious choice on a 24GB card, and the MTP drafters are a free latency win most people never enable."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Kimi K2.7 Code is the recommendation for almost nobody as a local model, and a serious option as an API with an open-weight escape hatch. Its value to a local-first developer is optionality rather than deployment - the knowledge that the weights exist under a permissive license, that self-hosting is possible if the economics ever justify eight GPUs, and that the vendor knows it."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The broader point is that the open-weight ecosystem has split into two tiers that do not compete with each other. One tier runs on hardware you already own and is improving fast enough that the gap to frontier closed models keeps narrowing for practical coding work. The other tier is open in license and closed in practice, gated by hardware rather than by terms. Knowing which tier you are shopping in is most of the decision."},{"type":"cta","title":"Google AI for Developers: Gemma 4 Model Overview","text":"Official documentation for the five Gemma 4 sizes, quantization options, multimodal support, and MTP speculative decoding.","buttonText":"Open the Gemma 4 Docs","buttonUrl":"https://ai.google.dev/gemma/docs/core"},{"type":"cta","title":"Ollama Model Library","text":"Verify the actual package size and available tags before you pull. Why the Math Changed in 2026"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Three shifts made the “just pay for Cursor” default weaker. First, frontier models became available through standard APIs at transparent token prices; the proprietary agent no longer owned unique model access. Second, open agents matured their tool-calling, approval gates, and multi-file edit loops to the point where the UX gap narrowed for most daily work. Third, local and open-weight models improved enough that a well-tuned 32B–70B class model running on a single high-end GPU can handle a large fraction of routine agent tasks without any cloud spend."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The remaining advantages of the best paid tools are polish, integrated indexing, and sometimes higher autonomy ceilings. The Agents That Actually Work Right Now"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Not every starred repository is production-ready. The following five consistently appear at the top of independent rankings and practitioner reports in 2026."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"OpenCode — the default open terminal agent"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"OpenCode (anomalyco/opencode) has become the most-starred open-source coding agent, with well over 200 k GitHub stars. It is MIT-licensed, provider-agnostic (75+ providers including local models), and ships a polished TUI. It supports parallel sessions, LSP-aware context, and can even authenticate with existing GitHub Copilot or ChatGPT subscriptions in some configurations. For developers who live in the terminal and want maximum model freedom, it is currently the strongest pure open-source default."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Cline — the VS Code agent with approval gates"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Cline remains the most-installed open-source agent inside VS Code. Apache-2.0 licensed, it emphasizes human-in-the-loop control: the agent plans, proposes edits and terminal commands, and waits for explicit approval. It supports a wide range of providers, strong MCP integration, and can run fully air-gapped when pointed at local models. If your requirement is “Cursor-like agency without leaving VS Code and without a subscription,” Cline is the practical answer for most teams."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Aider — git-native pair programming"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Aider is the veteran. It lives in the terminal, treats every change as a clean git commit, and is ruthlessly token-efficient. Apache-2.0, works with any OpenAI-compatible endpoint or Ollama, and produces the cleanest history of any open agent. It is less “autonomous multi-hour agent” and more “surgical pair programmer that never leaves a mess.” For staff engineers who value reviewable diffs over flashy autonomy, Aider remains unmatched."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"OpenHands — autonomous, self-hostable platform"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"OpenHands (formerly OpenDevin) is the most ambitious open platform. MIT-licensed, it runs agents in sandboxed environments with browser, terminal and editor tools. It is designed for longer autonomous runs and can be self-hosted, including air-gapped patterns. Teams that want ticket-to-PR style delegation without sending code to a third-party cloud increasingly evaluate OpenHands as the open alternative to fully managed agents."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Continue — IDE chat + autocomplete without lock-in"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Continue.dev occupies the “open Copilot” niche. VS Code and JetBrains extensions, full BYOK, excellent local-model support via Ollama, and configurable context providers. It is less of a full autonomous agent and more of a high-quality, fully controllable assistant layer. Many developers run Continue for daily completions and chat while using Cline or OpenCode for heavier agentic tasks."},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260912-155315-d39d364cf0eeceed.webp","alt":"Comparison matrix of OpenCode, Cline, Aider, OpenHands and Continue across interface, autonomy, license and local-model support","caption":"Figure 2: Quick decision matrix — pick by where you work (terminal vs IDE) and how much autonomy you actually want to grant.","altText":"Comparison matrix of OpenCode, Cline, Aider, OpenHands and Continue across interface, autonomy, license and local-model support"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"3. What You Still Give Up"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Honesty matters. Cline's own guidance on this is blunt: benchmarks show how models perform relative to each other, not how well they'll perform on your specific use case, and the only way to actually know is to contextualize benchmark results against the task in front of you rather than defaulting to whichever name is trending. That means a single task can genuinely use two different models: one that reasons carefully through the approach during planning, and a different one that executes the approved plan quickly and cheaply during implementation. This is genuinely one phase where paying for a premium, high-reasoning model pays for itself: the cost difference between a frontier reasoning model and a mid-tier one is small compared to the cost of building the wrong thing correctly. Once you move into active development — writing and iterating on the actual implementation — the priority shifts to real-world coding fluency: how well a model understands existing code patterns, follows established conventions in your codebase, and produces clean diffs rather than sprawling rewrites. By the time you reach deployment and review, the constraint shifts again — you're often asking a model to reason about a large amount of accumulated code at once, sometimes including screenshots or diagrams, so a large context window and multimodal support matter more here than raw reasoning depth.
